# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-16

| **[Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20597v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose \name, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, \name trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $τ^2$-bench, and AppWorld), \name (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.</p></details> |  |
| **[Price of Anarchy of First-Price Auctions in Agentic Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20587v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agentic market is a decentralized serving-time market where AI agents bid to claim each user prompt. An agent's bid is based on its expected value for the prompt, which is the expectation of the reward for its response minus its inference costs. First-price auctions (FPAs), in which the highest bidder pays its bid and receives the prompt, are a natural way to allocate prompts in such a market, since they are simple and transparent to run, and do not require central knowledge of private capabilities and costs. However, FPAs are not truthful: participants may bid less than their true value in the hope of a larger profit, and the prompt may then be allocated to someone other than the bidder who values it the most. This welfare loss is quantified by the $\textit{price-of-anarchy}$ (PoA). In this paper, we argue that agentic markets are likely to possess features that reduce the price-of-anarchy. The first is a $\textit{competitive floor}$, which occurs when multiple bidders usually value the prompt above some baseline share of the optimal welfare (e.g., because the best models are often distilled into cheaper alternatives, which perform similarly). We show that competition among them then limits the welfare loss, which can be driven to zero as the floor rises. The second feature is $\textit{near-independence}$ of values after conditioning on the prompt, which occurs when most of the predictable variation in the expected value is determined by the difficulty of the prompt. As the value distribution approaches conditional independence, we can derive an efficiency guarantee that approaches a factor of $1 - 1/e^2$ of the optimal welfare. Thus, both features give rise to welfare lower bounds that are significantly better than the well-known general case of $1 - 1/e$. We validate these results empirically using auctions with strategic bidders simulated on the EmbedLLM prompt routing dataset.</p></details> | 40 pages |
| **[One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20576v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training binds the competence of an LLM agent to the interface it was trained on. Semantics-preserving perturbations of that interface, such as renaming an action or changing the notation, leave the underlying task unchanged yet make the agent worse at it. Scoring each sample independently means interface randomization only broadens this dependence from a single interface to the training interface distribution, so out-of-distribution perturbations still degrade the agent. We propose Cross-Interface Policy Regularization (CIPR), a self-supervised objective that replaces action names with uninformative identifiers and presents each training sample under two interfaces in which equivalent actions are identified by construction. CIPR penalizes the KL divergence between the action distributions induced under the two interfaces, so the policy behaves consistently across them. We evaluate CIPR in both supervised and reinforcement-learning post-training, where it improves robustness. On agentic function calling, held-out interface perturbations reduce the accuracy of a post-trained agent by nearly 25%, and CIPR recovers 10.5% while leaving accuracy on the unchanged interface intact.</p></details> |  |
| **[Any Time, Any Space: A World-State-Centric Framework for Agentic Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20571v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-form agentic video generation must turn a screenplay into shots that remain spatially and temporally consistent across nonconsecutive requests. Passing prompts or recent frames between shots does not provide a shared spatial frame for revisiting a scene or a time-indexed record for replaying an earlier event. We present Any Time, Any Space (ATAS), which compiles a screenplay into shot requests with explicit location, camera, and event-time references. Before generation, it retrieves relevant evidence from a scene memory of registered static geometry and an event memory of dynamic observations indexed by scene, event, and time. After generation, ATAS reconstructs and registers the clip. It updates memory only when review and state checks accept the observation, while film delivery remains independent of memory admission. Across four video generators, ATAS improves all reported cross-shot consistency measures and achieves the highest aggregate benchmark score for each backbone. It also outperforms the compared agents and 3D-guided methods on target-image spatial revisit, leads three event-recapture criteria, and receives the highest human ratings on all six criteria. The video results and code are available on our project page: https://lhmd.top/atas/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lhmd.top/atas/, Code: https://github.com/ziplab/ATAS</p></details> |
| **[PhySGA: A Physics-Space Grounded Agentic Framework for Generalizable and Interactive 3D Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20560v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D object composition is pivotal to digital content creation and robot simulation, while also serving as a critical testbed for physical intelligence in foundation models. Previous methods either leverage large language models to directly predict object coordinates, or utilize generative diffusion priors to synthesize composite images for visual alignment, both of which rely on static priors in digital spaces. However, these methods consistently struggle with complex compositions such as precise hanging, gravity-driven settling and hidden containment, as these tasks require interactive construction processes involving global planning, fine-grained alignment, and physical simulation. To address these challenges, we propose PhySGA, a Physics-Space Grounded Agentic Framework for 3D composition via agent exploration and physical simulation, enabling the universal interactive assembly of rigid, articulated, and deformable objects. Directly applying vanilla LLMs or VLMs to this task proves insufficient, as current models inherently lack grounded physical understanding and interactive construction experience. To overcome this limitation, we design a collaborative feedforward-feedback architecture: an Assembly Agent focuses on geometric and kinematic reasoning for global step planning and coarse adjustment, while a Critic Agent reasons over contact dynamics for simulator state diagnosis and fine-grained closed-loop correction. Furthermore, Action Routers map semantic planning and analysis onto executable physical primitives within the simulator, while a task-specific Self-Evolving Skill Library distills historical trial-and-error feedback into reusable experiences, boosting success rates and minimizing exploration attempts. Extensive experiments within Isaac Sim demonstrate that PhySGA significantly surpasses existing methods in relation fidelity, physical plausibility, and visual quality.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://agenticei.github.io/PhySGA-page/</p></details> |
| **[EmbodiedEvo: Self-Evolving Coding Agent For Physical Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18867v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents can construct executable programs and revise them through feedback, offering a foundation for embodied control without target-task fine-tuning. Through a programmatic robot interface, the agent observes the scene, executes control programs, and adapts them to the resulting physical state, serving as the closed-loop policy. However, this within-episode adaptation does not automatically preserve experience across scenes. Our trajectory analysis shows that agents spend interaction and inference budgets establishing effective operations and identifying their preconditions, while fresh agents cannot reuse these discoveries. This motivates EmbodiedEvo, a self-evolving coding agent that distills physical feedback into reusable code Skills and diagnostic Lessons. Experience is acquired on one Playground scene per task, then frozen for independent agents in unseen scenes, without updating model weights. On RoboDojo-Sim, EmbodiedEvo achieves an average score of 44.48 and a success rate of 37.82\%, surpassing all officially verified results. Paired tests show the largest gains on memory-dependent tasks and successful reuse of coordinated procedures, while fine contact control remains difficult. Experience from stronger models also improves weaker executors: Astra's experience raises Sol and Luna's success rates by 8.86 and 5.50 percentage points over no experience. Compared with raw interaction history, Skills and Lessons achieve comparable task performance at 53.0\% lower execution cost.</p></details> |  |
| **[MOCHA: Multi-Objective Chebyshev Annealing for Agent Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19330v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents organize behavior through skills -- structured natural-language specifications that govern how an agent reasons, retrieves, and responds. Unlike monolithic prompts, skills are multi-field artifacts with platform limits: descriptions are indexed for routing, instruction bodies are loaded into a shared context window, and every extra line costs the agent context it could spend elsewhere. Skill optimization is therefore inherently multi-objective: a skill should be accurate and economical at once. Existing prompt optimizers collapse these goals into a single score, and we find that this makes them stall: in most of their runs, 1000 rollouts yield zero progress over the seed skill. We introduce MOCHA (Multi-Objective CHebyshev Annealing), which selects skills to improve by Chebyshev scalarization and accepts improvements that expand the Pareto front, annealing from exploration to exploitation. Chebyshev scalarization scores a skill by its largest weighted shortfall from a perfect skill; with random weights, it can select any skill on the Pareto front for improvement, including trade-offs that an average would never favor. Across six diverse skills and two models, MOCHA escapes the seed in nearly every run, achieving 7.9% higher mean accuracy than the strongest baseline (up to 23.7% on DebugBench with GPT 5.5 and 14.9% on FEVER with Claude Haiku 4.5) while discovering Pareto fronts with the highest hypervolume and nearly twice as many Pareto-optimal skills.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint. 24 pages, 20 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[SkillCommit: Evolving Agent Skills through Behaviorally Validated Scope Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15165v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents can continually improve without parameter updates by converting historical experience into reusable procedural knowledge. However, existing methods often consolidate experience based on semantic similarity or LLM judgments, which may merge superficially related but behaviorally incompatible strategies and thereby degrade performance. To address the issue, we propose SkillCommit, an online skill evolution framework that continuously transforms experience into a hierarchical library of reusable skills. Rather than generalizing from individual experiences immediately, SkillCommit first preserves each experience as an instance-specific patch, retaining its locally validated behavior. As patches accumulate, SkillCommit abstracts those sharing a common behavioral mechanism into higher-level skills. Specifically, for each incoming skill, embedding-based retrieval first identifies candidate related skills. Cross-instance replay and an LLM-based mechanism check determine whether these skills transfer across cases and share a common underlying mechanism. Candidates that pass both checks are abstracted into a higher-level skill and committed only if it preserves the validated behavior of all constituent skills. Experiments on RuleArena, OpenExempt and KOR-Bench demonstrate that SkillCommit consistently improves agent performance across diverse domains. Moreover, the learned skills transfer across model scales and families, enabling cross-model experience transfer.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic AI with Structured CoT for Enhancing AI's Spatial Intelligence: Visualization and Reasoning of Rotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04188v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent studies show that artificial intelligence (AI) with language and vision capabilities still experiences limitations in spatial reasoning. In this paper, we have studied the spatial capabilities of advanced generative AI to understand the rotations of objects in 3D space, utilizing AI's image processing and language processing features. We trained and examined the spatial intelligence of a generative Agentic AI model (GPT-5.6) to understand the spatial rotation process with rotation diagrams based on the revised Purdue Spatial Visualization Test: Visualization of Rotations (Revised PSVT:R). We improvised the Revised PSVT:R by superimposing additional graphical and contextual features to evaluate how different Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning strategies influence model performance. The results indicate that structured CoT reasoning improves the spatial reasoning performance of the base GPT-5.6 model in both datasets (PSVT:R and PSVT:R with coordinate system). We used three CoT approaches - (1) Structured CoT, (2) few-shot Structured CoT, and Structured CoT with Self-optimized Prompt. The three CoT approaches evaluated in this study showed no significant performance difference. Results showed that combining structured CoT reasoning with relevant contextual information leads to considerable improvements in VLM performance on 3D rotation tasks, demonstrating the potential of agentic AI for more effective spatial reasoning. However, when contextual information is removed, structured CoT reasoning alone provides limited improvement, and the models continue to exhibit notable difficulties in understanding spatial transformations. These findings suggest that effective spatial reasoning in VLMs relies on the integration of visual, textual, and reasoning-based information in future agentic AI systems for spatial intelligence.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Wealth of Agents: Scaling Agentic AI Across Developers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20518v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Today's agentic systems are typically designed, built, and deployed by single developers with full control over all participating agents. While this simplifies development, it limits the potential scale of agentic systems. We study multi-developer agentic systems (MDAs) which enable developers to collaboratively build agentic systems to push frontier capability beyond what any individual developer could achieve alone. We construct empirical and theoretical models of developers to compare MDAs with single-developer agentic systems (SDAs). On GAIA, a general assistant benchmark, a population of specialized developers whose individual agents do not exceed 35% accuracy reaches 70% when their agents are combined in an MDA. This MDA performs 12 percentage points above the median general-purpose SDA built with several times each specialist's development budget. Our theoretical models show how MDAs can incentivize specialization and encourage more efficient use of aggregated developer resources. Put together, our results make a preliminary case for MDAs as a new scaling axis by enabling developers with diverse capabilities and resources to advance agentic AI collaboratively.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning From a Steady Hand: A Weakly Supervised Agent for Robot Assistance under Microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20776v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper rethinks steady-hand robotic manipulation by using a weakly supervised framework that fuses calibration-aware perception with admittance control. Unlike conventional automation that relies on labor-intensive 2D labeling, our framework leverages reusable warm-up trajectories to extract implicit spatial information, thereby achieving calibration-aware, depth-resolved perception without the need for external fiducials or frame-by-frame manual depth annotation. By explicitly characterizing residuals from observation and calibration models, the system establishes a task-space error budget from recorded warm-ups. Under the evaluated laboratory conditions, the recorded trajectories yielded an empirical lateral error level of approximately 49μm and a conservative axial error estimate of approximately 291 μm during large in-plane moves. In a within-subject user study (N=8), the learned agent reduces overall NASA-TLX workload by 77.1% relative to the simple steady-hand assistance baseline. Together, these results demonstrate successful closed-loop micromanipulation and reduced subjective workload under the evaluated laboratory conditions, without external fiducials or frame-by-frame manual depth annotation, supporting the proposed approach as a framework for microscope-guided robotic assistance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in IEEE Transactions on Robotics (T-RO), 2026</p></details> |
| **[RoboSimStudio: An Interactive Toolkit for Human and Agent Rapid Real2Sim of Robot Manipulation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18835v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>It has been difficult to accurately model the physics of the interaction between a robot and the objects it manipulates, including contact mechanics, coupled contact dynamics, deformations, stiffness and friction. We present RoboSimStudio (RSS), an interactive toolkit that makes choices of solver, contact model, and physical parameters explicit and editable, allowing both humans and coding agents to quickly create and iteratively refine simulations based on observed physical behavior. We implement RSS with the open Newton physics simulation system and describe experiments with human-guided real2sim using (1) visual evaluation, (2) force-feedback and (3) agentic Real2Sim2Real. Code, video and additional information can be found on the project website: https://robosimstudio.github.io</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 7 figures, 1 table. Project website: https://robosimstudio.github.io</p></details> |
| **[AdaRSI: On-Demand Recursive Self-Improvement for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20474v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pretrained vision-language-action models (VLAs) often fail midway through unseen long-horizon tasks. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) offers a remedy, but embodied RSI methods either refine the harness around a frozen executor and so cannot add a missing motion, or train the executor on persistent failures without asking whether a failure lies in how the executor is called or in what it can do. We ask when a self-improving embodied system should change the weights of its executor, and propose AdaRSI, an on-demand RSI framework for embodied agents. A coding agent first improves its skills and harness, then attributes persistent failures by retrying failing sub-steps from saved states, and fine-tunes only the sub-steps that remain unreliable, with demonstrations it collects itself and experience carried over from earlier tasks. On all 50 target tasks of RoboCasa365, with GR00T N1.5 as the executor and no human demonstrations, AdaRSI improves the VLA on all three suites and raises its success on unseen composite tasks from 4.6% to 13.8%. Compared with fine-tuning every sub-step, it reaches slightly higher success while using only 28% of the GPU time and 22% of the self-collected demonstrations, and fine-tuning without a harness trains more yet gains far less.</p></details> |  |
| **[Same Claim, Different Interpretations: How LLM Agents Resolve Ambiguity in Scientific Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20457v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Co-scientists and agentic scientific claim verifiers are increasingly used to assess claims across the sciences. Natural-language scientific claims, written for human experts, are often underspecified and so admit several defensible interpretations. Such verifiers usually pick one, often tacitly, and benchmarks score their verdicts against expert labels without recording the expert's interpretation or the verifier's in a comparable form. This leads to disagreements with expert labels that cannot be traced to the evidence or the interpretation, and to compute spent on unchecked interpretations. We introduce Claim Cards to make such interpretations explicit in the reasoning itself. A Claim Card records a verifier's interpretation as nine fields before evidence gathering, each marked stated, inferred, or default. To test whether an agent's interpretation can change its verdict, we ran a GPT-5.4 agent five times on each of 60 expert-labeled materials-science and biomedical claims with a card, without one, and with one card frozen across runs (900 runs). Over 40% of card fields were not marked stated, and a card added about a tenth to a run's GPT-5.4 tokens and time without measurably changing agreement with expert labels. The agent's own interpretations varied between runs, although within a claim this variation did not measurably track the verdict. A deliberate change to one field of the frozen card, however, making the quantifier universal or the threshold for support stricter, changed the verdict on about a third of the claims, against about one in seven expected by chance (p < 0.001 for each edit). On 24 materials-science claims run with computational tools, the stricter threshold also changed what the agent asked those tools to compute. We therefore argue that verdicts should be reported with their interpretations and that benchmarks could publish the interpretation behind each label.</p></details> | 51 pages, 7 figures |
| **[HarnessSQL: Harness-Native Training for SQL Agents in Realistic Database Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12274v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-SQL models are commonly trained to map questions directly to static queries, whereas real-world database agents operate through stateful, multi-turn interaction with live databases -- inspecting schemas, executing probe queries, diagnosing errors, and revising hypotheses. This creates a critical train-deploy mismatch, as the execution harness that mediates this interaction is introduced only at inference time. To bridge this gap, we propose HarnessSQL, a harness-native post-training framework that preserves the full interaction structure throughout both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. HarnessSQL builds isolated, executable database environments paired with hidden execution oracles, rolls out teachers directly inside the target SQL harness, and retains only verified trajectories for full-sequence SFT, followed by execution-reward RL. Across Spider 2.0-SQLite, HarnessSQL dramatically boosts the execution accuracy of compact models, raising Qwen3-8B from 15.5% to 45.2% and Qwen3-14B from 22.2% to 54.8%, while transferring effectively to out-of-distribution interactive benchmarks such as BIRD-Interact and LiveSQLBench. Our findings demonstrate that training database agents directly within their execution harness is essential for mastering complex, long-horizon database workflows.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Agentic Robots: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20442v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models are driving a paradigm shift in robotic intelligence, moving beyond low-level action prediction to goal-directed orchestration of data collection, model training, task planning, and real-world execution. We characterize this emerging paradigm as an agentic robots, in which agents reason over human instructions, interact with robotic environments, rectify execution errors autonomously, and dynamically adjust operational strategies. Although this field develops fast, current studies are not well connected. Researchers have no consistent view of how to combine different agentic functions into a complete robot system. This paper provides a unified overview of existing research on agentic robots. We first trace the development from robot policies to agentic systems, covering vision-language-action models, world models, and world-action models. We then clarify the definition and scope of agentic robots and organize existing research into seventeen categories across four functional domains: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness. Across these domains, we examine how agents shape model learning and robot decisions, collect and curate robot data, construct worlds, tasks, and tests for learning and evaluation, and organize capabilities for robot use. Finally, we discuss open challenges and outline directions for future research. Looking forward, we hope this work contributes to a clearer understanding of agentic robots and supports new approaches to making them more capable, reliable, and adaptive.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Training to Escalation: Forensic Analysis and Local Recreation of RL/RLHF-Induced Privilege Escalation in AI Agents (CVE-2026-65616)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20437v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mid-2026 reporting on autonomous AI evaluation incidents claimed that frontier large language model (LLM) agents escaped server containment through emergent coordination and cloud infrastructure attacks. This paper contextualizes those claims through forensic analysis and containerized recreation of both the Highly Persistent Internal Model (HPIM) training-case conditions and the Phase One privilege escalation targeting internal package repositories during OpenAI's ExploitGym evaluations. We recreate the configuration of the OpenAI-provided training example and reproduce the cryptographic verification bypass (CVE-2026-65616) in isolated repository servers, where misconfigured systems enabled token forgery and privilege escalation through basic HTTP requests, Python, and JavaScript. Crucially, the training-example conditions and the Phase One (and later) misconfigurations share the same exploitable patterns. Our analysis explains how Zero Trust Architecture principles neutralize this shared attack vector. Contemporary AI risks derive directly from training and deployment conditions in ambivalent learning environments lacking adequate containment measures.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 1 figure. Code and replication environments available at https://github.com/alixiacf/hpim-training-lab DOI:10.5281/zenodo.22817059 and https://github.com/alixiacf/rep-openai-artifactory DOI:10.5281/zenodo.22817059</p></details> |
| **[SwarmRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through Evolving Scientific Agent Swarm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20431v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in scientific research aims to improve agents' research capabilities through their own experimental experience. Scientific agent swarms can combine evidence and implementations from different research directions to guide their agents' subsequent experiments. However, poor experimental results can leave unclear whether to revise a hypothesis or its implementation, while overall program scores can hide components useful to other agents. To address these challenges, we introduce SwarmRSI, a framework that evolves scientific agent swarms for recursive self-improvement. We devise Feedback-Guided Swarm Mutation to refine hypotheses and their implementations using experimental feedback from other agents. We also propose Complementary Swarm Recombination to reuse retained hypotheses and complementary program components across agents, allowing partial results to support new research. Recursive Swarm Evolution integrates both mechanisms into a feedback loop, using new experimental evidence to guide subsequent research. Experiments on OpenProblems, NatureBench and MLE-Bench Lite demonstrate SwarmRSI's state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. Further evaluations show that accumulated research experience improves performance on held-out tasks. Biologists also reproduce its programs for follow-up biological analyses.</p></details> |  |
| **[BizBench: Unveiling Hidden Challenges in Deploying Long-Horizon Agents for Real-World Business](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20424v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing long-horizon agent benchmarks often emphasize final task success while providing limited assessment of intermediate execution validity. Yet real-world business operations require agents to navigate extensive, potentially outdated or conflicting policies while satisfying critical constraints throughout execution. These requirements are particularly consequential in high-stakes domains such as payments, where intermediate violations can incur financial losses or regulatory consequences regardless of the final outcome. To bridge this evaluation gap, we introduce BizBench, a multi-turn benchmark grounded in real-world procedures from a leading payments company. Its executable environment integrates 789 knowledge documents and 601 discoverable business tools, with consistent policies, states, and tool behavior. The benchmark comprises 50 tasks involving resource constraints, temporal dependencies, prior commitments, and conflicting or outdated guidance. Each task is validated through reference execution, while deterministic checks assess both goal attainment and execution validity without prescribing a single solution path. Experiments reveal substantial limitations even among frontier models: Claude Opus 5 and GPT-6 Sol achieve strict success rates of only 64.0% and 61.3%, respectively. For Qwen3.8-27B, goal attainment reaches 42.0%, but strict success drops to 32.7%, demonstrating how outcome-based evaluation can mask invalid execution. Beyond success rates, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash averages 191.3 tool calls per run, approximately 70% more than reference executions, revealing substantial execution overhead. Overall, BizBench establishes a testbed for evaluating and advancing long-horizon agents beyond task completion, toward reliable and constraint-compliant execution in complex business environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evaluating LLMs as Agentic Workflow Executors in Real-World Customer Support Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14346v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise customer-support agents increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) to execute bounded, tool-using workflows. We study this problem through the lens of Skill LLMs: specialized executors that operate within a selected skill context and must complete a narrow, stateful workflow under policy and tool constraints. We present a benchmark construction pipeline that mines recurrent workflows from real-world transcripts, converts them into structured skill descriptions, maps them to tool requirements, simulates environment state with Model Context Protocol (MCP)-compatible mocks, and generates multi-turn tool-using dialogues. Using this benchmark, we conduct a large-scale zero-shot evaluation of 17 language models spanning closed model families and deployment-oriented open-weight models from 1.7B to 14B parameters, each judged independently by five strong LLM judges, plus a deterministic rule-based evaluator that checks tool coverage, hallucinated tool use, and unsupported claims. We find a consistent ranking in which GPT-5.4 leads by a wide margin and QWEN3.5-9B is the strongest open-weight model (below 20B), outperforming several closed baselines under every judge. We also find that judge choice is not a negligible methodological detail: pairwise judge agreement on the pass/fail verdict averages Kappa=0.524 across 10 judge pairs. These results suggest that comprehensive zero-shot evaluation of skill execution requires judge reliability as carefully as model capability.</p></details> |  |
| **[What Should a Software Team Spend on Agents? Simulation-Based Cost Optimization of Mixed AI-Human Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20394v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Context: AI agents are rapidly adopted in software development, but their token costs are rising and it is unclear how much a team should spend on agents, and on which tasks. Objective: We determine how teams should divide spending between human developers and AI agents to deliver the features a project demands at the lowest total cost. Method: We model mixed teams of human developers and AI agents with eight specialized roles as a discrete-time stochastic process covering bug injection and detection, technical debt, human supervision of agents, and release cycles with feature freezes. Using Monte Carlo simulation and evolutionary search, we determine cost-optimal teams for 432 scenarios varying developer capability, project velocity, debt dynamics, agent capability and price, and bug costs, and compare them with a human-only baseline. Results: Escaped bugs are the dominant cost driver, and 61% of the agent budget goes to finding bugs. Agent capability matters more than token price, since higher-quality outputs reduce downstream quality assurance costs. In teams with capable agents, human developers mostly fix bugs and leave the implementation of new features to the agents. Teams with high-capability agents are typically cheaper than human-only teams, but low-capability agents offer no consistent advantage. At realistic velocities, the optimal teams still spend more on developers than on agents. Conclusions: There is neither a universal token budget per developer nor a universal agentic team structure. As in traditional development, this depends on many factors and teams need to be carefully tailored to the project context to achieve the optimal cost-quality trade-off.</p></details> |  |
| **[STP-GUI: Spatially-Aware Token Pruning for Efficient Web-GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20390v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the rapid development of vision-language models (VLMs), Web-GUI agents have achieved promising performance in interacting with real-world interfaces through screenshot-based grounding. On where: conventions depend on what the player already knows about the card, not on how recently it was hinted, and they are used most in the mid-game; humans use them earlier and later in the game than the agents. Interaction helps only with a misinformed majority, recovering up to 0.24 accuracy over voting (0.29 under consensus). A one-sentence unreliability warning recovers up to 0.18 accuracy under voting (0.28 under consensus), at no significant cost under voting when context is true. Robustness should be judged against exposure-matched baselines, with prompt-level skepticism as cheap first defense.</p></details> | <details><summary>64 pa...</summary><p>64 pages, 12 figures, 31 tables</p></details> |

Further analysis shows that epistemic uncertainty serves as a calibrated difficulty detector, with highest-uncertain agents exhibiting 14.8 times the combined failure rate across collision, off-road, and DNF, of lowest-uncertain agents, enabling automatic prioritization of challenging scenarios without ground-truth labels.</p></details> |  |
| **[New Model, Old Habits: Behavior Transfer under Model Swapping in Multi-Session LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19712v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-session agents can switch between LLMs to balance performance and inference cost. Such switches require transferring a history for continuity, yet their effects on the recipient remain underexplored. Through controlled experiments with multi-session tasks, we uncover behavior transfer: recipients tend to adopt reasoning and action patterns from the models that generated their histories. This effect is most evident with raw-history transfer but is substantially reduced by summarization. Based on these findings, we propose Direction- and Authorship-aware Summarization of History (DASH) that preserves stronger-model behavior while minimizing weak-to-strong transfer via summarization. Experiments with repeated swaps show that DASH outperforms raw-history transfer and naive summarization baselines. These results suggest that effective multi-model orchestration requires considering not only which model to use, but also which behavioral patterns to carry forward.</p></details> | 27 pages |
| **[RuleTrek: Benchmarking Agentic Rule Reasoning in Complex Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19698v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly tackle complex real-world tasks in specialized domains. To address this gap, this paper introduces \textbf{RuleTrek}, a multi-domain benchmark for agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios. Its rule-grounded construction pipeline extracts structured rule graphs, generates and incrementally composes rule-specific sub-scenarios while ensuring consistency and solvability. To systematically evaluate agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios, RuleTrek assesses three capabilities in interactive workspaces: Rule Identification (RI), Rule Deduction (RD), and End-to-End Rule Reasoning (E2E). Experiments on 11 frontier LLMs show that the highest RI and RD scores are only 62.47\% and 60.43\%, respectively, while E2E scores range from 26.97\% to 55.29\%. These results demonstrate that current LLM agents face substantial challenges in rule reasoning over complex scenarios. We make our code and dataset publicly available at \href{https://github.com/yuan247/RuleTrek}{RuleTrek}.</p></details> |  |
| **[Interlock: From Local Correctness to Task Completion in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19686v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents increasingly undertake long-horizon tasks in complex environments characterized by fragmented evidence, partially completed states, and dependencies across resources and artifacts. To address this completion gap, we propose Interlock, a framework for task synthesis that represents each task as a typed completion graph. Grounding edges connect public evidence to decisions, coupling hyperedges impose joint constraints across components, and realization edges connect decisions to all required downstream effects. Interlock compiles this graph into a resettable workspace, a public request, and an evaluation rubric. A source-coverage review checks whether the original requirements survive generation, while a public-input review checks whether the rubric is supported by the materials available to the solver. An agent-as-judge inspects execution artifacts and runs verification scripts, retaining trajectories judged to satisfy all mandatory requirements. To evaluate training utility, we fine-tune two Qwen3.6 backbones on their own accepted trajectories. Across the two backbones, training improves AutomationBench task pass rates by 4.00-4.50 percentage points, JobBench weighted scores by 3.20-6.64 points, and Harvey LAB criterion pass rates by 3.89-4.16 percentage points. | **[MCAIR: Monitoring and Correcting Agent Unfaithfulness via Internal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19679v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in completing complex tasks through multi-step reasoning and tool interactions. However, recent studies have revealed that LLM-based agents can exhibit unfaithful behaviors, where their final reports fail to faithfully reflect their actual execution trajectories. We refer to this specific form of agent unfaithfulness as Agent Completion Unfaithfulness. Detecting and mitigating agent completion unfaithfulness is critical yet remains largely unexplored. In this work, we propose MCAIR, a runtime monitoring and correction framework that leverages internal representations of LLM-based agents. MCAIR uses multi-step internal representations as inputs to train a risk-aware probe, enabling online detection of potential completion unfaithfulness during execution. Furthermore, MCAIR introduces a risk-guided correction loop that prompts agents to review their execution history and mitigate potential inconsistencies between their execution trajectories and final reports. Additionally, we construct ACUD, a dataset for Agent Completion Unfaithfulness detection that provides step-level execution traces, step-level internal representations, and consistency annotations for training and evaluating runtime detection probes. Experiments demonstrate that MCAIR outperforms LLM-as-a-judge baselines, generalizes across prompts and tasks, and effectively reduces agent completion unfaithfulness.</p></details> | 26 pages |
| **[ABot-ER: Unified Embodied Reasoning through Agentic Data-Model Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18355v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce ABot Embodied Reasoning (ABot-ER), a unified embodied reasoning foundation model that breaks the tension between breadth and depth in embodied reasoning. Within a single unified model, ABot-ER commands specialist-level depth across all three competencies, spanning spatial, scene, and temporal reasoning in perception; long-horizon action organization in planning; and point grounding and affordance in execution. | **[OmniStepBench: Deployment-Aware Agent Evaluation across Modalities and Task Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06059v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models evolve from question-answering systems into general-purpose agents, evaluation must move beyond static answer correctness to assess multimodal perception, multi-step execution, tool use, and artifact delivery. | **[ANCHOR-RE: An Agentic Biomedical Relation Extraction Framework with Retrieval Augmentation and Error-Driven Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03154v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical relation extraction (BioRE) supports knowledge graph construction and hypothesis generation. | **[ANCHOR: Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16859v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For repository-grounded coding agents, a functionally correct patch is not necessarily an acceptable one: developers also constrain how the task must be carried out-modification scope, workflow order, validation, tool use, and reporting. | **[Model What Matters: Decision-Guided Selective World Modeling for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19642v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning provides a direct mechanism for optimizing task objectives in large language model-driven agents, but task rewards are typically sparse trajectory-level signals that only evaluate task success, without directly supervising action-conditioned environment transitions, leaving the model with limited explicit understanding of action consequences and environment dynamics. DGS-WM employs a Decision-Guided Observation Selection component that filters observation units with decision value as world modeling targets based on their support for subsequent policy responses, and a Confidence-Adaptive Weighting component that adaptively adjusts supervision strength according to the model's prediction confidence on selected targets. Together, these components form a decision-aware auxiliary world modeling loss, jointly optimized with the RL loss on the same policy model. Across multiple benchmarks covering single-hop/multi-hop question answering and interactive web tasks, DGS-WM achieves consistent improvements under different model scales and RL algorithms, demonstrating more stable and effective performance compared to full-observation world modeling methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>Core ...</summary><p>Core implementation included as ancillary files</p></details> |
| **[SEDM: Selective Self-Evolving Distributed Memory for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09498v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents in a society can reuse each other's experience, but indiscriminate memory use increases processing cost and can reduce task success. | **[J-tau: A Fully Localized Japanese Benchmark for Tool-Agent-User Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19638v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present J-tau, the first publicly available Japanese benchmark for evaluating tool-using LLM agents in interactive customer-support scenarios. Yet existing systems and evaluations largely rest on an implicit perfect-colleague assumption: collaborators provide correct and complete information and respond promptly to requests. To separate task-solving competence from the ability to recover an unreliable evidence-gathering process, we introduce the Unreliable Colleagues Benchmark (UnCoBench), which organizes behavioral failures through Misaligned, Incomplete, and Missed settings. The main agent need to verify contextual correctness, diagnose information gap and track unresolved dependencies in order to obtain the correct evidence for solving the task. We also introduce a verifiable and scalable automated generation pipeline that pairs LLM-based scenario generation with executable checks of answer uniqueness and role necessity. Results across ten models show that similar reliable-setting performance can result in different robustness to unreliable evidence. Trace analysis reveals frequent omissions of corrective follow-up, and the recovery behavior involves inefficient waiting and redundant queries. These findings show that robust collaboration requires not only sound reasoning, but also targeted action to diagnose evidence failures, recover relevant information, and integrate it into decisions. ## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Large Scale Investigation of Scaling Limits in Chemical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13408v3)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemical Language Models (CLMs) are increasingly used in de novo drug design, driven by recent growth in model scale, compute, and dataset size. However, the relationship between design choices, training dynamics, and downstream generation quality remains poorly understood. We present a compute-controlled scaling study of CLMs comprising more than 30,000 experiments across molecular representations (SMILES, SELFIES, SAFE), tokenizations (atom-level and byte-pair encoding), model scales (0.5M-1B parameters), leakage-controlled datasets (MOSES, ChEMBL, PubChem, ZINC-22), and architectures (decoder-only and encoder-decoder). By fitting IsoFLOP profiles, we establish clear scaling trends in pretraining loss, but find that these improvements do not translate into comparable gains in goal-directed molecular design. Layer-wise probing and sparse autoencoder analysis reveal continued development of chemical representations: chemical syntax saturates early, while semantic properties emerge more slowly and become increasingly accessible with further training and model scale. These representational gains coexist with diminishing improvements in goal-directed generation under the evaluated protocols and oracle budgets. Our resulting suite of models, NovoMolGen, achieves state-of-the-art results, outperforming prior CLMs and specialized generative models in goal-directed molecular generation across drug discovery tasks. These findings expose a disconnect between chemical representation learning and downstream molecular design, motivating the development of pretraining paradigms that more directly learn chemical semantics.</p></details> |  |
| **[One Sequence, Many Decodings: CAGenMol-2 Recasts Drug Design as Masked Molecular Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34301v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug design couples property evaluation, conditional generation, structure-based design, and local optimization, yet machine learning systems typically address these capabilities with separate task-specific models. We introduce CAGenMol-2, a masked diffusion molecular language model that represents molecules, continuous scalar properties, and 3D protein pockets within a single wrapped sequence. Within this pretrained interface, downstream operations are selected by which sequence regions are observed or masked at inference, allowing one checkpoint to perform property prediction, property- and pocket-conditioned generation, and partial-constraint design without task-specific architectures or backbone fine-tuning. We further propose Adaptive Fragment Optimization (AdaFO), a gradient-free mask-and-refill search that turns the masked decoder into an iterative local molecular optimizer. On CrossDocked2020, AdaFO increases Success Rate from 30.2\% to 70.8\%, the best reported under this protocol, while largely preserving drug-likeness and diversity. Finally, scaffold-preserving directional editing and CRBN/VHL case studies demonstrate its use in compound design workflows spanning local molecular editing, structure-based prioritization, and downstream simulation-based screening.</p></details> |  |
| **[PocketVE: Stable and Property-Guided Structure-Based Drug Design with Variance-Exploding Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08101v3)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-conditioned 3D molecule generation is a central challenge in structure-based drug design, requiring a balance between pocket compatibility, molecular properties, and physical geometry. We propose \textbf{PocketVE}, a protein-pocket-conditioned variance-exploding (VE) diffusion framework that couples stable coordinate denoising with inference-time property guidance. Specifically, PocketVE combines an EDM-style training and sampling setup for 3D denoising, classifier-free guidance for multi-property steering, and adaptive protein perturbation as a training-time pocket regularizer.On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark under GenBench3D, PocketVE improves Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ from 58.6 to 80.6 and reduces strain energy from 457.4 to 127.9 relative to its guided TAGMol architectural parent; relative to TargetDiff, it attains comparable Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ with lower strain energy (127.9 vs.\ 306.0), while retaining competitive docking and molecular-property scores under moderate guidance. A guidance-scale study shows that moderate guidance gives a favorable balance between target-related objectives and geometric quality, whereas stronger guidance can degrade geometry and distributional fidelity. Pocket-permutation and PoseCheck diagnostics further support pocket-specific spatial compatibility with reduced steric conflicts. Overall, the results suggest that geometric stability and inference-time property guidance should be considered as coupled design objectives.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by Neurips 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Systematic Evaluation of Molecule Generation Models for De Novo Drug Design: From Benchmarks to Practical Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10099v1)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecule generation has emerged as a powerful computational tool for de novo drug design, enabling the exploration of chemical space beyond the limits of conventional virtual screening. The field has progressed rapidly, driven by advances in molecular representations, generative architectures, and target-aware modeling strategies. However, existing reviews typically address specific model families or application scenarios in isolation, rather than offering an integrated perspective on how these components collectively form a coherent generation workflow. In this review, we present a comprehensive evaluation of molecule generation models for de novo drug design, covering 82 methods across five deep generative frameworks, including recurrent neural network (RNN)- and Transformer-based models, variational autoencoders (VAEs), generative adversarial networks (GANs), flow-based models, and diffusion models. We first summarize widely used benchmarks and molecular representations, and then examine the methodological principles underlying both general and pocket-conditioned generation. A central contribution of this work is a systematic synthesis and comparative analysis of reported performance across commonly used benchmarks and evaluation metrics. We also summarize representative experimentally validated case studies. Looking ahead, we discuss future directions in standardized 3D data, interaction-aware generation, receptor flexibility, and multi-objective molecular design, with the aim of improving the reliability and experimental relevance of molecule generation. All collected benchmark resources, evaluation metrics, and model references are provided in a publicly accessible repository at https://github.com/JacklinGroup/molecule-generation-review.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This document is the unedited Author's version of a Submitted Manuscript subsequently accepted for publication in [Journal of Chemical Information and Modeling]. To access the final published article</p></details> |
| **[FuseDiff: Symmetry-Preserving Joint Diffusion for Dual-Target Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05567v2)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dual-target structure-based drug design aims to generate a single ligand together with two pocket-specific binding poses, each compatible with a corresponding target pocket, enabling polypharmacological therapies with improved efficacy and reduced resistance. Existing approaches typically rely on staged pipelines, which either decouple the two poses via conditional-independence assumptions or enforce overly rigid correlations, and therefore fail to jointly generate two target-specific binding modes. To address this, we propose FuseDiff, an end-to-end diffusion model that jointly generates a ligand molecular graph and two pocket-specific binding poses conditioned on both pockets. FuseDiff features a message-passing backbone with Dual-target Local Context Fusion (DLCF), which fuses each ligand atom's local context from both pockets to enable expressive joint modeling while preserving the desired symmetries. Together with explicit bond generation, FuseDiff enforces topological consistency across the two poses under a shared graph while allowing target-specific geometric adaptation in each pocket. To support principled training and evaluation, we derive a dual-target training set and use an independent held-out test set for evaluation. Experiments on the benchmark and a real-world dual-target system show that FuseDiff achieves state-of-the-art docking performance and enables the first systematic assessment of dual-target pose quality prior to docking-based pose search.</p></details> | KDD 2026 |
| **[NEAT-POCKET: Pocket-Conditioned Autoregressive 3D Molecular Generation with a Neighborhood-Guided Set Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05097v1)** | 2026-09-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-driven de novo molecular design offers a promising route to accelerate early-stage drug discovery by generating novel ligands directly within target protein binding pockets. We present NEAT-POCKET, a pocket-conditioned extension of the autoregressive NEAT model for 3D molecular generation. NEAT-POCKET generates molecules atom by atom in protein pocket environments while preserving atom permutation invariance and explicitly modeling hydrogen atoms. Benchmarks on the CrossDocked and SPINDR datasets show that NEAT-POCKET achieves competitive structure-based generation performance while sampling substantially faster than existing baselines. Here, we leverage low-resolution electron density (ED) derived from the filler as a physically grounded condition for \textit{de novo} drug design. We consider two types of ED, calculated and cryo-EM/X-ray, obtainable from computational or experimental sources, supporting unified pre-training and experimental integration. Compared with rigid pocket representations, experimental ED naturally captures conformational flexibility and provides a more faithful description of the binding environment. Based on this, we introduce EDMolGPT, a decoder-only autoregressive framework that generates molecules from low-resolution ED point clouds. By grounding generation in physically meaningful density signals, EDMolGPT mitigates structural bias and produces molecules with 3D conformations. Evaluations on 101 biological targets verify the effectiveness. Our project page: https://jiahaochen1.github.io/EDMolGPT_Page/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published as a conference paper in ICML 2026</p></details> |
| **[On the Collapse of Generative Paths: A Criterion and Correction for Diffusion Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10339v2)** | 2026-06-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inference-time steering adapts pretrained diffusion and flow models to new tasks without retraining, often utilizing ratio-of-densities constructions that reweight time-indexed marginals with fixed exponents. We identify Marginal Path Collapse, a failure mode in which the intermediate density defined by such compositions becomes non-normalizable despite valid endpoints. This collapse can arise when composing heterogeneous experts trained with mismatched noise schedules (and/or negative exponents / partial supports). To address this, we provide (i) a sharp sufficient Path Existence Criterion that characterizes when the composed intermediate densities are mathematically well-defined, and (ii) Adaptive Path Correction with Exponents (ACE), which generalizes Feynman-Kac steering to support time-varying exponents. Our analysis reveals that ACE controls the quantile radius of the intermediate distributions, providing a theoretical mechanism for path stabilization observed in experiments. On flexible-pose scaffold decoration, a drug design task composed of de-novo, conformer, and protein-conditioned experts, ACE prevents collapse and significantly outperforms constant-exponent baselines. Furthermore, ACE improves attribute success rates in compositional image generation, establishing it as a general framework for compositional sampling. Project Page: https://ziseoklee.github.io/projects/ACE/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICML 2026</p></details> |
| **[Demystifying Multimodal Biomolecular Co-design With Intrinsic Geodesic Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01628v1)** | 2026-06-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomolecules such as proteins and small-molecule ligands play a central role in biological systems, arising from the tight interplay between sequence and three-dimensional structure. We argue that a critical but overlooked degree of freedom lies in how these marginal processes are temporally coupled during training and generation, where inappropriate coupling can introduce high-variance supervision and inconsistent intermediate states, affecting modality consistency. To address this, we introduce GeoCoupling, a systematic framework that optimizes for temporal couplings between heterogeneous modalities. Empirical results across structure-based drug design and unconditional protein design demonstrate the learned couplings consistently outperform synchronous and randomly coupled baselines, yielding biomolecules with improved physical validity and diversity.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICML 2026</p></details> |
| **[Fine-Tuning Diffusion Models for Molecular Generation via Reinforcement Learning and Fast Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01220v1)** | 2026-05-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating molecules that simultaneously satisfy drug-like properties and conform to the 3D structure of a target protein is a core challenge in structure-based drug design (SBDD). Each specification in turn constrains the conditional information supplied to a neural-based generator, which rejects any instance violating the symbolic specification. Like other neurosymbolic approaches, SNG exploits the complementary strengths of symbolic and neural methods. The outcome of an SNG is a pair $(H, X)$, where $H$ is a symbolic description of feasible instances constructed from data, and $X$ a set of generated new instances that satisfy the description. Joint generation and affinity prediction enable inference-time scaling through importance sampling, steering design toward higher-affinity compounds. Case studies validate this: selective CK2$α$ ligand generation against CLK3 shows significant correlation between predicted and quantum-mechanical binding energies. Scaffold elaboration on ER$α$, TYK2, and BACE1 demonstrates strong agreement between predicted affinities and QM calculations while confirming geometric fidelity. Unlike traditional flow models that operate in sample space, MolPIF interpolates between distributions in the parameter space, theoretically recovering Wasserstein-2 optimal transport for continuous coordinates and establishing Fisher-Rao geodesics for discrete atom types. Additionally, MolPIF exhibits versatility in lead optimization and offers flexible prior distribution selection (such as Laplace), establishing a robust paradigm for SBDD. To avoid the instantaneous trajectory collapse induced by geodesics directly targeting Dirac distributions, we propose Evolving Exponential Geodesic Flow for SBDD (EvoEGF-Mol), which replaces static Dirac targets with dynamically concentrating distributions and is trained with a progressive-parameter-refinement architecture. | **[SMDD-Bench: Can LLMs Solve Real-World Small Molecule Drug Design Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21740v2)** | 2026-05-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents have incredible potential for scientific discovery applications. | **[SciCore-Mol: Augmenting Large Language Models with Pluggable Molecular Cognition Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22287v1)** | 2026-05-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are central to the one-for-all intelligent paradigm, but they face a fundamental challenge when dealing with heterogeneous scientific data such as molecules: the inherent gap between discrete linguistic symbols and topological molecular or continuous reaction data leads to significant information loss and semantic noise in text-based reasoning. Preprint</p></details> |

While machine learning force fields (MLFFs) can sample stable conformations by relaxing molecular geometries according to physical forces, they require costly ab-initio training data. In this work, we propose generative pseudo-force fields (GPFFs) to bridge these paradigms by training an MLFF on a quadratic pseudo-potential energy surface relative to reference equilibrium structures. Because no ab-initio calculations are required for the perturbed geometries, non-equilibrium training data can be generated on the fly by perturbing the equilibria with Gaussian noise. We show that GPFFs constitute a time-step-agnostic variant of variance exploding DMs: the score comes from the predicted pseudo-forces but because force magnitudes implicitly encode the noise level, no time-step conditioning is needed. Our GPFF can hence be used as a drop-in replacement in standard diffusion sampling (ancestral, Heun) but also facilitates more efficient, adaptive variants and an MLFF inspired direct denoising scheme. Our proposed sampling algorithms support arbitrary structural priors and geometric constraints. On QM9, GPFF has 100 % validity at 256 neural function evaluations (NFE) and over 50 % at just 6 NFE, outperforming diffusion baselines across all samplers. | **[Reading the Cell, Designing the Cure: Perturbation-Conditioned Molecular Diffusion for Function-Oriented Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15243v1)** | 2026-05-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When reliable target structures are unavailable at scale or phenotypes arise from dysregulated pathways, transcriptomic perturbations provide a system-level functional readout for drug action. | **[ToolMol: Evolutionary Agentic Framework for Multi-objective Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12784v2)** | 2026-05-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Advances in large language models (LLMs) have recently opened new and promising avenues for small-molecule drug discovery. Multi-objective Bayesian optimization is a promising solution for such expensive, black-box problems: it fits probabilistic surrogates and selects new designs via an acquisition function that balances exploration and exploitation. In practice, it requires tailored choices of surrogate and acquisition that rarely transfer to the next problem, is myopic when multi-step planning is often required, and adds refitting overhead, particularly in parallel or time-sensitive loops. We present TAMO, a fully amortized, universal policy for multi-objective black-box optimization. TAMO uses a transformer architecture that operates across varying input and objective dimensions, enabling pretraining on diverse corpora and transfer to new problems without retraining: at test time, the pretrained model proposes the next design with a single forward pass. We pretrain the policy with reinforcement learning to maximize cumulative hypervolume improvement over full trajectories, conditioning on the entire query history to approximate the Pareto frontier. Across synthetic benchmarks and real tasks, TAMO produces fast proposals, reducing proposal time by 50-1000x versus alternatives while matching or improving Pareto quality under tight evaluation budgets. These results show that transformers can perform multi-objective optimization entirely in-context, eliminating per-task surrogate fitting and acquisition engineering, and open a path to foundation-style, plug-and-play optimizers for scientific discovery workflows.</p></details> |  |

In each task, models are tested on molecules that were unseen during training, but the training data is chosen such that generalisation to the test examples should be feasible for models that learn the underlying physical principles.
